\title{Human-Realistic Golfer: Calculation-Level Reference\\[4pt]
\large Coordinate Conventions, Sign Map and Human-Range Baseline (HRG-00)}
\author{UpstreamDrift Estimation Working Reference}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
Epic \#13160 asks that the best tour-average driver fit stay inside human
joint ranges without losing its marker fit.  Before any model change can be
judged, the 44 coordinates of the full-body v1 model need a pinned definition:
which axis each one turns about, and what a positive value means anatomically.
This reference records that definition as a table measured from compiled
geometry (not from comments), the basis map to ISB, the baseline violations of
the human ranges by the current best fit, and the exact reproduction commands.
Each later HRG child appends its own section here, including failed
experiments.  Nothing in this document qualifies the model scientifically.
\end{abstract}

\tableofcontents

\section{Status, Scope and Governance}

This is an editable standalone research and experiment record in the sense of
\texttt{AGENTS.md} (``Modeling Reference Documentation''); it is not the
engineering design manual, whose only editable source is
\texttt{manuals/upstreamdrift}.  It follows
\path{docs/research/model_aware_matching/model_aware_matching.tex} in
style.  Software correctness is recorded here; scientific qualification stays
in the design-manual governance pathway.

\paragraph{Status of This Revision (Truthful).}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item Measured: axes and signs of all 44 coordinates in the
    hip-calibrated anthropometric family (\S\ref{sec:signmap}), against
    compiled MuJoCo, and re-asserted from Drake and Pinocchio forward
    kinematics.
  \item Measured: the baseline range violations of the private best-fit
    bundle (\S\ref{sec:ranges}).
  \item Convention, not measurement: neutral forearm pronation at $q=0$
    (\S\ref{sec:wrist}).  The specification has no palm or thumb landmark.
  \item Placeholders: grip, appearance, pelvis and hip centres, segmental trunk
    and anatomical angles.  No fix is claimed here.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Reproduction (Private Paths Described Generically).}
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
  \item Hash the private spec and bundle with \texttt{sha256sum}; compare with
    the table above.
  \item Sign map:
{\small
\begin{verbatim}
pytest -o addopts="" -q -n 0 \
  tests/unit/motion_matching/test_full_body_coordinate_sign_map.py
\end{verbatim}}
  \item Range report (set the variable to the private bundle path):
{\small
\begin{verbatim}
HRG_BEST_FIT_BUNDLE=<private-bundle> pytest -o addopts="" -q -n 0 -s \
  tests/unit/motion_matching/test_bundle_human_range_characterisation.py
\end{verbatim}}
  \item Headless environment:
{\small
\begin{verbatim}
QT_QPA_PLATFORM=offscreen MPLBACKEND=Agg MUJOCO_GL=egl PYTHONPATH=.:src
\end{verbatim}}
\end{enumerate}

\section{Coordinate Conventions and Sign Map}
\label{sec:signmap}

\paragraph{Frames and Method.}
The model frame at $q=0$ is the pelvis frame: $x$ forward, $y$ left, $z$ up
(right-handed).  For each coordinate the test compiles the specification to
MuJoCo, sets that coordinate to $+10^{\circ}$ (or $+10$\,mm for the slides) with
all others zero, and records (i) the rotation axis of the child body, from
$R_1R_0^{\top}$ expressed in the pelvis frame, and (ii) the motion of a probe
point fixed in the child body $0.1$\,m from the joint anchor along the first
of forward, up, left that is not within $60^{\circ}$ of the axis.  The
perturbation is a single hinge, so the result is the axis and the sense of a
positive increment.  Drake and Pinocchio forward kinematics give the same
axes to within $0.5^{\circ}$.  The table is
\path{src/shared/python/motion_matching/coordinate_sign_map.py}.

\subsection{Wrist Labels and Forearm Pronation}
\label{sec:wrist}

The panel disagreed on \texttt{LWInputX} versus \texttt{LWInputY}.  The probe
answers it, in the rolled (\texttt{WRIST\_ROLL}) family:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \texttt{*WInputX} turns about $-y$, exactly parallel to the elbow axis
    ($|\hat a_{\mathrm{wrist}}\cdot\hat a_{\mathrm{elbow}}|=1$).  A positive
    value lifts the forward hand, the way elbow flexion does (elbow $+$ lowers
    it).  It is therefore cock: radial deviation, with ulnar negative.  This
    matches \texttt{HUMAN\_RANGES\_DEG} ($-40..25$).
  \item \texttt{*WInputY} turns about $+z$, perpendicular to the elbow axis and
    to the forearm.  It is flexion/extension.  The axis and the probe motion
    ($+y$, left) are the same on both sides, which is opposite on the two
    hands: extension on the left (dorsum faces $+y$) and flexion on the right
    (palm faces $+y$).  The symmetric range $\pm70$ hides this.
  \item \texttt{*FInput} turns about $-x$, the proximal direction of both
    forearms.  A positive value is supination on the left and pronation on the
    right.
\end{itemize}
These labels assume that the forearm is neutral (thumb side toward the elbow
flexion side) at $q=0$.  That is the convention \texttt{WRIST\_ROLL} and the
range table are written for; the geometry confirms the axis relations but not
the convention, because the specification carries no palm landmark.  If the
zero forearm were supinated, \texttt{*WInputX} would be flexion and
\texttt{*WInputY} deviation.

\subsection{Trunk Order}
Order, proximal to distal: \texttt{TorsoInput} (about pelvis $z$), then
\texttt{SpineInputX}, \texttt{SpineInputY}.  The test twists by $30^{\circ}$ and
finds the tilt axes yawed to $(\cos30^{\circ},\sin30^{\circ},0)$ and
$(-\sin30^{\circ},\cos30^{\circ},0)$, while the twist axis stays at the pelvis
$z$ axis for any tilt.  A positive twist turns the upper torso to the left
(counterclockwise seen from above); a right-handed backswing is negative.

\subsection{Spec Family: Was the Wrist Roll Applied?}
Two families exist.  Native specs (\path{full_body_spec_v1.json}, \path{full_body_spec_v2.json},
primitives Rx, Ry) keep the native wrist base, whose second primitive spins
about the forearm and is redundant with pronation.  Anthropometric specs
(primitives Rx, Rz) use $\mathrm{WRIST\_ROLL}\cdot R_{\mathrm{native}}$ with
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{WRIST\_ROLL}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\-1&0&0\\0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
\texttt{wrist\_roll\_applied(spec, native)} returns true, false, or raises
\texttt{ValueError} for any other basis.  The compiled geometry agrees: the
dot product of the second wrist axis with the forearm axis is 1 for native
specs and 0 for rolled ones.  Comparing the cock axis with the elbow axis does
not separate the families, because the native elbow is itself tilted.

\section{ISB Basis Map}
\label{sec:isb}

ISB (Wu et al.) uses $X$ anterior, $Y$ up, $Z$ right.  With the model basis
above, a vector $v=(x,y,z)$ becomes
\begin{equation}
v_{\mathrm{ISB}}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\0&0&1\\0&-1&0\end{pmatrix}}_{M}v,
\qquad M^{\top}M=I,\ \det M=+1,
\end{equation}
and a rotation $R$ becomes $MRM^{\top}$, which preserves angle and
handedness.  A left twist (positive about model $z$) is positive about ISB $Y$.
Implemented and tested as \texttt{model\_to\_isb}, \texttt{isb\_to\_model},
\texttt{model\_rotation\_to\_isb} in \texttt{coordinate\_sign\_map.py}.

\label{sec:ranges}

The report-only test
\path{test_bundle_human_range_characterisation.py} prints, per coordinate,
the minimum and maximum of \texttt{reference\_q} (degrees, wrapped to
$\pm180^{\circ}$) against \texttt{HUMAN\_RANGES\_DEG}.  It never fails.  Applied
to the private bundle (1815 frames, 32 ranged coordinates), 12 coordinates
exceed their range with no tolerance:

\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrr@{}}

\texttt{RWInputX} & $-53.4$ & $12.8$ & $-40$ & $25$ \\
\texttt{RWInputY} & $-74.4$ & $49.3$ & $-70$ & $70$ \\

The four values quoted by the panel (\texttt{LWInputX}, \texttt{RWInputX},
\texttt{hip\_rotation\_r}, \texttt{hip\_rotation\_l}) are reproduced exactly.
The repository helper \texttt{violations()} applies a $0.5^{\circ}$ tolerance,
so it reports fewer marginal cases (\texttt{LEInput}, \texttt{NeckInputZ},
\texttt{mtp\_angle\_*}); the report here is strict on purpose.  Marker RMS of
the same fit is 50.93\,mm, the number later children must hold while the
violations go to zero.

\section{Grip}

\subsection{Subject Handedness and Lead-Trail Sides (HRG-GR0, \#13201)}

\paragraph{Definition.}
\texttt{subject.handedness} is \texttt{"right"} or \texttt{"left"}: the hand that
is \emph{lead} (nearer the target) is the anatomical left hand of a right-handed
golfer and the right hand of a left-handed golfer. \texttt{core.handedness} is the
single resolver: \texttt{lead\_side} yields \texttt{"L"} for right and \texttt{"R"}
for left, \texttt{trail\_side} the opposite, and any other value raises \texttt{ValueError}.
A specification without the field resolves to \texttt{"right"} through
\texttt{subject\_handedness}; this is a declared default, not an inference from data.
The builder writes the key only for a non-default value, so every specification and
receipt committed before this change remains byte-identical. The receipt schema records
an optional \texttt{handedness} field that is \texttt{null} on legacy receipts.

\paragraph{Sign Conventions and Sagittal Reflection.}
\texttt{GripPose.landmarks} uses the golfer body frame with $x$ anterior, $y$ toward
the golfer's anatomical left, and $z$ up. The sagittal plane is $x$--$z$, so the lateral
axis is $y$ and the sagittal reflection is $P = \mathrm{diag}(1, -1, 1)$. The target
lies toward $+y$ for a right-hander and $-y$ for a left-hander; the toe axis is $+x$;
the grip axis is idealized as vertical, down $-z$ from the butt. Every distance and
angle of the grip is expressed in lead/trail terms and is identical for both handednesses;
only the target direction, hence the azimuth and palm-normal vectors, changes sign in $y$.
Consequently, $P\,\ell_{\mathrm{right}} = \ell_{\mathrm{left}}$ for each landmark $\ell$,
asserted within $10^{-9}$~m.

\subsection{Cross-Model Grip Capability Inventory (HRG-GS-M0, \#13322)}
To support the cross-engine grip contact and hand-coupling objectives of epic
\#13297, a machine-readable capability manifest is established at
\texttt{src/config/grip\_capability\_inventory.json} under schema
\texttt{grip-capability-inventory/v1}. The inventory classifies every model asset
across four capabilities:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \texttt{visual\_style}: Rendering of hand-hand grip overlap, interlock, or ten-finger configurations.
\item \texttt{allocated\_hand\_hand}: Static or kinematic allocation of dual-hand wrenches onto bilateral hand-hand pairs.
\item \texttt{physical\_pad\_grip}: Dynamic contact between designated anatomical hand pads and the club grip cylinder.
\item \texttt{physical\_hand\_hand}: Dynamic inter-hand pad-to-pad contact dynamics.
\end{enumerate}

Every entry records its verified mechanical attachment mode and its finger
kinematic fidelity.  The attachment modes are
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \texttt{rigid\_branch\_kinematic}, \texttt{closure\_weld}, \texttt{site\_weld},
    \texttt{mocap\_weld} and \texttt{direct\_mid\_hands};
  \item \texttt{bushing\_compliance} and \texttt{free\_plus\_bushings};
\end{itemize}
and the finger fidelities are \texttt{none}, \texttt{rigid\_proxy} and
\texttt{articulated}.
By contract, no capability may claim \texttt{available = true} without an active
\texttt{evidence\_test} verifying the code path; currently, only the full-body-v1
spec export path (exercised in \texttt{test\_engine\_grip\_contact.py} and
\texttt{test\_opensim\_contact\_grip.py}) provides physical pad-grip contact
evidence. All other capabilities remain \texttt{available = false} with explicit
diagnostic reasons and tracking issues mapped to epic children (\#13326--\#13336).

Models that carry no hands are explicitly segregated in an \texttt{excluded}
list with formal justifications: \texttt{putting\_green},
\texttt{putting\_green\_gui}, \texttt{pendulum\_simulator} and
\texttt{swing\_objective\_lab}.

Empirical verification against source assets revealed several divergences from
initial panel assumptions:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{MyoSuite sim repository}: Panel notes assumed \texttt{shared/models/myosuite/myo\_sim}
held an active 23-DOF hand model; verification confirmed the path is an unpopulated checkout directory,
\texttt{myoarm\_simple.xml} lacks hands, and \texttt{golfer\_myobody\_driver.xml} utilizes rigid
ellipsoid hands with site-welds.
\item \textbf{MuJoCo golfer kinematics}: \texttt{golfer.xml} attaches the club shaft as a direct
kinematic child of \texttt{hand\_left}, leaving \texttt{hand\_right} entirely uncoupled without equality welds.
\item \textbf{Drake URDF topology}: The club shaft is rigidly fixed to \texttt{right\_hand} with no finger
links, leaving \texttt{left\_hand} disconnected.
\item \textbf{Pinocchio URDF topology}: The club shaft attaches to a \texttt{mid\_hands} frame connected
directly to \texttt{thorax3}, bypassing both left and right hand branches.
\end{itemize}

A Design-by-Contract loader at
\texttt{src/shared/python/grip\_contact/capability\_inventory.py}
strictly validates the manifest against disk paths, schema versioning, non-empty diagnostics,
and tracking issue conventions.

\subsection{Grip-Style Contact Topology (HRG-GS-G1, \#13299)}
\textbf{Contract.} \texttt{GripStyle} (overlap, interlock, ten-finger) and
\texttt{trigger\_finger} are now physical members of \texttt{GripSpec}; a
non-default value is written by \texttt{apply\_grip\_config} as a new spec
revision and changes the physics hash.  The default (overlap, no trigger) is
omitted from the physical JSON, so existing v1 spec bytes are unchanged.

\textbf{Pick classification and sidecar migration (\#13346).} Because
\texttt{grip\_style} selects the physical contact topology (which finger sites
contact the grip cylinder and which hand--hand pairs exist),
\texttt{grip\_selection.py} classifies \texttt{grip\_style} in
\texttt{PHYSICAL\_PICKS} ($9 \to 10$ physical picks) and removes
\texttt{grip\_style}/\texttt{"style"} from \texttt{VISUAL\_PICKS} and
\texttt{VISUAL\_KEYS} ($5 \to 4$ visual picks: \texttt{grip\_thumb\_offset\_deg},
\texttt{grip\_strength\_deg}, \texttt{lead\_roll\_offset\_deg},
\texttt{trail\_roll\_offset\_deg}).  Picking a non-default style on an
unrevised v1 document mints \texttt{spec\_revision.version = 1} with
\texttt{configuration = "grip"}, stamps \texttt{subject.grip\_config.style} and
\texttt{subject.grip\_provenance}, and moves \texttt{physics\_spec\_sha256}
(e.g., on the anthropometric driver spec, \texttt{style\_only}
\texttt{interlock} moves \texttt{physics\_spec\_sha256} from
\texttt{296bdc363b3f\dots} to \texttt{98bd6b2e7957\dots} with
\texttt{refit\_required: false} $\to$ \texttt{true}).  Re-picking the
document's current physical style (including \texttt{"overlap"} on an unrevised
v1 document) is a no-op that leaves the document bytes and hash identical.
New \texttt{appearance-v1} sidecars never carry \texttt{grip.style};
\texttt{migrate\_sidecar\_grip} strips a legacy sidecar \texttt{grip.style}
with a \texttt{DeprecationWarning} when it matches the document's physical
style and raises \texttt{ValueError} naming both styles when it disagrees, so
sidecar and physical styles can never diverge.  On \texttt{native\_viewer\_export},
\texttt{--grip-style} is refused with a message pointing to \texttt{spec\_builder}.

\textbf{Frame.} Sites are expressed in the shared grip frame of
\texttt{grip\_contact.pad\_layout}: $+x$ along the grip from butt to head,
$\rho=\sqrt{y^2+z^2}$ radial.  Each site is a sphere or capsule
(centre, unit axis, half-length, radius).

\textbf{Pair table.} Every style has eight pad--grip pairs (palm and finger,
proximal and distal, per hand), the lead heel pad on the butt region and the
trail palm on the lead thumb (sphere--capsule).  Overlap adds the trail little
finger on the lead index (capsule--capsule); interlock adds the trail little
finger against the lead middle finger and the lead index wrapping the trail
little finger (two capsule--capsule pairs); ten-finger puts the trail little
finger on the grip beside the lead index (capsule--cylinder).  The optional
trail index trigger adds a capsule--cylinder pair.

\textbf{Anatomy.} Radii scale linearly with hand length $L$
($L_0=0.19$\,m, valid $0.14\le L\le0.24$\,m): little finger
$0.0075\,L/L_0$, index $0.008\,L/L_0$, thumb $0.0095\,L/L_0$, heel pad
$0.012\,L/L_0$\,m.  These are anthropometric assumptions, not measurements.

\textbf{Physics honesty.} In \texttt{full-body-v1} the lead hand is the club
body and the trail hand is welded to it, so every hand--hand pair is
\emph{allocated} (reporting only) in weld, closure, bushing and contact modes;
it is physical only in the separated-hand v2 topology (HRG-GS-G11, \#13309).

\textbf{Acceptance.} Exact pair sets per style; every site within 0.5\,mm of
the grip surface or its partner at the neutral pose; radii proportional to
$L$; one \texttt{GripStyle} definition in \texttt{src/}; \texttt{grip\_style}
classified as physical across CLI, desktop and web with legacy sidecar
migration/refusal.  Reproduce with:
\begin{verbatim}
python3 -m pytest tests/unit/grip_contact/test_contact_topology.py \
    tests/unit/motion_matching/test_grip_config.py \
    tests/unit/motion_matching/test_grip_selection.py
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Limitation.} No finger markers exist in the capture, so finger contact
locations are not identifiable from data; the table fixes them by assumption.

\subsection{Allocated Hand--Hand Contact Reporting (HRG-GS-G3, \#13301)}
\textbf{Method.} We generalize the palm--thumb allocation (\texttt{palm\_thumb.py})
across every hand--hand pair in the contact topology $\mathcal{T}$ (HRG-GS-G1,
\#13299). Given the world-frame closure wrench $F_{\mathrm{closure}} =
(f_{\mathrm{lead}}, \tau_{\mathrm{lead}})$ from the trail-hand-to-club
constraint and hand--club reactions $(f_{\mathrm{hc}}, \tau_{\mathrm{hc}})$,
the residual wrench $F_{\mathrm{rem}} = (f_{\mathrm{lead}} -
f_{\mathrm{hc}}, \tau_{\mathrm{lead}} - \tau_{\mathrm{hc}})$ is
partitioned among active hand--hand pairs via weights $w_i \ge 0$ ($\sum_i w_i =
1$). The split across pairs is an explicit, labelled modelling prior
(\texttt{AllocationPrior}), not an identified physical quantity. Hand--club reactions
$(f_{\mathrm{hc}}, \tau_{\mathrm{hc}})$ default to zero when not supplied, which attributes
the entirety of the observable closure wrench to hand--hand pairs, exactly as HRG-H1 did.
\texttt{AllocatedHandContacts} records the active \texttt{prior\_name} and
\texttt{hand\_club\_source} (\texttt{"supplied"} vs \texttt{"not\_supplied"}) so this
modelling assumption is visible to all consumers. For each pair $i$, the patch point $p_i$
and unit normal $\hat{n}_i$ (pointing lead to trail) are evaluated via primitive distance
solvers. The force $f_i = w_i f_{\mathrm{rem}}$ is transported to $p_i$, resolving
couple $c_i = w_i \tau_{\mathrm{rem}} - (p_i - p_{\mathrm{ref}})
\times f_i$. Compressive normal force is $f_{n,i} = \max(0, -f_i \cdot
\hat{n}_i)$, tangential force $f_{t,i} = \|f_i - (f_i \cdot
\hat{n}_i)\hat{n}_i\|$, and friction ratio $\mu_{\mathrm{eff}} =
f_{t,i}/(\mu f_{n,i})$. Unloaded flags mark tensile configurations ($-f_i
\cdot \hat{n}_i \le 0$).

\textbf{Parameters.} Static friction coefficient $\mu = 0.9$; equal-split pair
weights prior, $w_i = 1/N_{\mathrm{pairs}}$ (\texttt{name="equal\_split"});
normal tolerance $10^{-9}$; minimum closure force $10^{-9}$\,N.  Hand--club
reactions default to zero, recorded as
\texttt{hand\_club\_source="not\_supplied"}.

\textbf{Acceptance Criteria.}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item The sum of allocated hand--hand forces and hand--club forces reproduces closure
$\lambda$ to $\le 10^{-9}$\,N.
\item Every allocated channel emits \texttt{method="allocation"}, user-visible
label \path{external:<pair>_allocated}, \path{prior_name}, and
\path{hand_club_source}; requesting physical mode on welded
topology raises \path{SchemaFeatureUnavailableError} via \path{require_allocated_mode}.
\item Native viewer overlay renders one arrow per allocated pair under the allocated
contact toggle.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Limitations.} Allocation partitions a single resultant closure wrench
using an assumed modelling prior rather than solving multi-body compliant contact; internal squeeze co-contraction
between opposing pairs is not observable from the net closure wrench. Hand--club forces default to zero when not supplied.

\textbf{Reproduction Command.}
\begin{verbatim}
python3 -m pytest tests/unit/grip_contact/test_allocated_hand_contact.py \
    tests/unit/grip_contact/test_palm_thumb.py
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Topology-Driven Finger Pose and Visual Roll (HRG-GS-G4, \#13302)}
\textbf{Method.} Visual finger placement in \texttt{model\_appearance} is driven directly by \texttt{ContactTopology} contact sites instead of the legacy \texttt{\_FINGER\_LINKS} string table. The lead index finger, trail little finger, and optional trail index trigger are placed by inverting the three-phalanx parametric wrap so that the distal phalanx centroid matches the corresponding topology site centre to within $\le 1$\,mm while ensuring that non-penetrating grip pad clearance is maintained. Per-hand visual roll rotates each hand body about the grip axis by its respective roll offset $\alpha$, absorbing issue \#13166.

\textbf{Parameters.} The topology site centre $\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{site}} = (x, y, z)$ in the shared grip frame defines the axial target $z_{\mathrm{hand}} = x - d_{\mathrm{butt}}$, radial target $\rho = \sqrt{y^2+z^2}$, and azimuthal target $\theta = \operatorname{atan2}(z, y)$. For unlinked fingers, nominal curl angles are computed from $V$-azimuth $v = 20^\circ + \alpha$ and knuckle offset $110^\circ$. For topology-driven fingers, a 1D monotonic solve determines tangent radius $d$ and metacarpophalangeal azimuth $\theta_{\mathrm{mcp}}$ such that the distal phalanx vertex centroid achieves sub-millimetre agreement with $\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{site}}$. Thumb azimuth is shifted by $\alpha$ to preserve hand-rigid rotation. Symmetric barrel ring stations $(0.2, 0.4, 0.6, 0.8)$ are added alongside the tangent-point station on each phalanx capsule so that relocating the linked finger onto the partner hand leaves no un-sampled barrel gap across the remaining proximal phalanges, raising 36-bin world azimuthal wrap coverage (lead/trail) from $34/36$ ($0.9444$) and $32/36$ ($0.8889$) on driver overlap, $34/36$ ($0.9444$) and $31/36$ ($0.8611$) on iron7 overlap, $33/36$ ($0.9167$) and $34/36$ ($0.9444$) on driver and iron7 interlock, $34/36$ ($0.9444$) and $35/36$ ($0.9722$) on driver ten-finger, and $34/36$ ($0.9444$) and $34/36$ ($0.9444$) on iron7 ten-finger to full $36/36$ ($1.0000$, all 36 bins covered) for every club and style.

\textbf{Acceptance Criteria.} (1) Rendered fingertip positions match \texttt{ContactTopology} site centres to $\le 1$\,mm across all styles (overlap, interlock, ten-finger) and trigger mode; (2) Overlap, interlock, and ten-finger produce visibly distinct finger geometries with appropriate \texttt{on\_grip} flags and match golden mesh summaries; (3) Per-hand roll follows $\alpha$ to $\le 0.1^\circ$ across fingertips, palm, and thumb; (4) The duplicate \texttt{\_FINGER\_LINKS} table is eliminated from \texttt{grip\_pose}.

\textbf{Reproduce.} Reproduce with:
\begin{verbatim}
python3 -m pytest tests/unit/model_appearance/test_grip_finger_topology.py \
    tests/unit/model_appearance/test_grip_hand_mesh.py \
    tests/unit/model_appearance/test_grip_pose.py
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Limitations.} Hand phalanges remain rigid capsules wrapped on the grip cylinder; individual phalanx flexion dynamics during the swing are visual approximations rather than musculoskeletal coordinate solves.

\subsection{Named Grip Presets (HRG-GS-G5, \#13303)}
\textbf{Method.} Three historical and coaching consensus two-handed grip presets
---\texttt{palmer\_overlap}, \texttt{woods\_interlock}, and
\texttt{ten\_finger}---are formalised over \texttt{GripSpec}, parameterising
inter-hand linking style, golf angles ($\alpha, \psi, \gamma$) for both hands,
hand spacing, and trigger-finger extension. The numeric values are
\texttt{photo\_estimate} assumptions, not measurements; no image is committed
in the repository. Every parameter records \texttt{photo\_estimate} provenance
in \texttt{subject.grip\_provenance}, distinguishing photo-estimate assumptions
from direct kinetic or marker measurements.

\textbf{Parameters.} The panel-consensus starting values and actually consulted
qualitative sources are:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Palmer Overlap}: \texttt{GripStyle.OVERLAP}, lead
    $\alpha=+12^{\circ}$, $\psi=45^{\circ}$, $\gamma=0^{\circ}$; trail
    $\alpha=+12^{\circ}$, $\psi=35^{\circ}$, $\gamma=0^{\circ}$; spacing
    $0.074$\,m; trigger finger on. Qualitative source: golf.com's published
    excerpt from the book \emph{Arnold Palmer: American Hero}. Palmer describes
    an overlap grip, both Vs pointing at the right shoulder seam (strong-ish),
    the right lifeline folding over the left thumb, a slight right-index trigger,
    and a palm-finger hold for the left hand. Evidence still requirement: face-on
    high-speed camera still required at address showing the trail index trigger
    finger extended down the shaft, the lead thumb nested in the trail thenar pocket,
    and the trail little finger riding over the lead index knuckle.
  \item \textbf{Woods Interlock}: \texttt{GripStyle.INTERLOCK}, lead
    $\alpha=+2^{\circ}$, $\psi=38^{\circ}$, $\gamma=0^{\circ}$; trail
    $\alpha=+2^{\circ}$, $\psi=34^{\circ}$, $\gamma=0^{\circ}$; spacing
    $0.069$\,m; trigger finger off. Qualitative source: an interlock grip seen
    in published photos of Tiger Woods: a neutral lead hand with about 2 to 2.5
    knuckles showing, the trail little finger hooked between the lead index and
    middle fingers, and the right palm over the left thumb. Evidence still
    requirement: face-on high-speed camera still required at address showing
    the trail little finger deeply interlocked between the lead index and middle
    fingers, 2 to 2.5 knuckles visible on the lead hand, and the right palm
    covering the left thumb.
  \item \textbf{Ten-Finger}: \texttt{GripStyle.TEN\_FINGER}, lead
    $\alpha=+5^{\circ}$, $\psi=40^{\circ}$, $\gamma=0^{\circ}$; trail
    $\alpha=+5^{\circ}$, $\psi=32^{\circ}$, $\gamma=0^{\circ}$; spacing
    $0.090$\,m; trigger finger off. Qualitative source: general instructional
    description with no named person: all ten digits making direct contact with
    the grip cylinder without overlap or interlock, mild rotation (+5$^{\circ}$ on
    both hands), lead palm diagonal 40$^{\circ}$, trail finger diagonal 32$^{\circ}$,
    90\,mm hand spacing, and no trigger-finger separation. Evidence still
    requirement: face-on camera still required at address showing all eight fingers
    and two thumbs directly contacting the grip wrapper, with the trail little
    finger flush adjacent to the lead index finger without overlap or interlock.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Acceptance.} Presets load across CLI (\texttt{--grip-preset}), desktop
(\texttt{CharacterBuilderModel}) and web API surfaces (\texttt{/api/character-builder/grip}),
round-tripping through \texttt{apply\_grip\_config} with provenance
\texttt{photo\_estimate}, never \texttt{measured}. All angles remain strictly
within the GR1 anatomical bounds. Feature parity registry entry
\texttt{tools.character\_builder\_grip} is updated. Reproduce with:
\begin{verbatim}
python3 -m pytest tests/unit/grip_contact/test_named_presets.py \
    tests/unit/motion_matching/test_grip_selection.py
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Limitation.} Numeric values are \texttt{photo\_estimate} assumptions,
not measurements, and no image is committed in the repository. Starting angles
and spacing represent photographic pose estimations rather than dynamic
motion-capture measurements; individual grip variations across club selections
or swing phases are unmodelled.

\subsection{Pinocchio Topology Contact Reference (HRG-GS-G6, \#13304)}
\textbf{Topology Contact Model.} The engine-agnostic contact model (\path{src/shared/python/grip_contact/topology_contact.py})
evaluates compliant physical contact between anatomical hand sites $\mathcal{S}$ defined in \texttt{ContactTopology} $\mathcal{T}$
(HRG-GS-G1, \#13299) and the club grip cylinder via the G2 pairwise kernels (\path{pair_contact.py}, \#13300).
Sites ride on the prescribed hand frame, while the club grip cylinder rides on the dynamic club frame.
Physical hand--grip contact pairs are evaluated using \texttt{sphere\_capsule} for spherical sites (palm and finger pads, heel pad)
and \texttt{capsule\_capsule} for cylindrical finger segments (such as the optional trail index trigger finger); since \#13308
\texttt{capsule\_capsule} is the axial line-contact law of Section~\ref{sec:hrg-gs-line-contact}.

For each physical pair $i$, the signed penetration depth $\delta_i = \max(0, r_{A,i} + r_{B,i} - d(p_A, p_B))$ and relative contact point
velocity $v_{c,i}$ yield compressive normal force $f_{n,i}$ and regularized friction force $f_{t,i}$:
\begin{align}
f_{n,i} &= k \delta_i \left(1 + d \dot{\delta}_i\right) \hat{n}_i, \\
f_{t,i} &= -\mu f_{n,i} \, \mathrm{step}\left(\frac{\|v_{t,i}\|}{v_{\mathrm{trans}}}\right) \frac{v_{t,i}}{\|v_{t,i}\|},
\end{align}
where $\hat{n}_i$ is the outward unit normal on the club grip cylinder at the closest point, $v_{t,i} = v_{c,i} - (v_{c,i} \cdot \hat{n}_i)\hat{n}_i$,
and $\mathrm{step}(x) = \min(1, x)$ is the Stribeck transition function.
The total hand wrench on the club in world axes about the club grip-frame origin matches the \texttt{PadWrench} convention:
\begin{equation}
F_{\mathrm{club}} = \sum_{i} f_{b,i}, \quad
M_{\mathrm{club}} = \sum_{i} \left[ m_{b,i} + (p_{c,i} - p_{\mathrm{club\_orig}}) \times f_{b,i} \right].
\end{equation}

\textbf{Coordinate Frames.} Sites in \texttt{ContactTopology} are defined in the shared butt-origin grip frame ($+x$ axial from butt to head,
$y=z=0$ along the shaft axis). In \texttt{GripInterface}, each hand's local grip frame origin lies along the shaft axis at the hand centre
$x_{\mathrm{orig}} = \frac{1}{2}(x_{\mathrm{palm\_proximal}} + x_{\mathrm{palm\_distal}})$, with transverse offset $[0, 0, -r_{\mathrm{grip}}]$
for the lead hand (Left) and $[0, 0, +r_{\mathrm{grip}}]$ for the trail hand (Right).
The helper \texttt{map\_site\_to\_hand\_frame} transforms site primitives into the hand frame:
\begin{equation}
p_{\mathrm{hand}} = p_{\mathrm{axis\_offset}} + \begin{bmatrix} x_{\mathrm{site}} - x_{\mathrm{orig}} \\ y_{\mathrm{site}} \\ z_{\mathrm{site}} \end{bmatrix}.
\end{equation}
At the weld pose, every physical pad--grip site's surface gap is exactly $-0.250$\,mm, satisfying the $\pm 0.5$\,mm neutral preload contract.

\textbf{Pinocchio Adapter.} \texttt{ClubInHands} in \path{src/engines/physics_engines/pinocchio/python/grip_contact_sim.py} accepts an optional
\texttt{topology\_model: TopologyContactModel}. When supplied, \texttt{wrenches()} evaluates \texttt{topology\_side\_wrench} and caches per-pair
wrenches in \texttt{last\_pads}. \texttt{simulate\_grip\_contact} integrates the club equations of motion using SciPy Radau implicit integration
and logs per-pair normal and tangential force traces in \texttt{ContactRun.pair\_normal\_force\_n} and \texttt{ContactRun.pair\_tangential\_force\_n}.
Unavailable channels (such as allocated hand--hand pairs) are recorded as \texttt{NaN} with explanatory reasons in \texttt{channel\_reasons}.

\textbf{Parameters.} Stiffness $k = 1.6 \times 10^5$\,N/m, dissipation $d = 0.5$\,s/m, static and dynamic friction coefficient $\mu = 0.90$,
velocity transition $v_{\mathrm{trans}} = 0.01$\,m/s, patch radius $r_{\mathrm{patch}} \approx 4.47$\,mm matched to $1100$\,N squeeze per hand.

\textbf{Acceptance Criteria.}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item Zero force at separation; unilateral normal force ($f_n \ge 0$).
\item Friction cone constraint: $\|f_t\| \le \mu f_n$ (with regularized Stribeck allowance).
\item Action--reaction balance: $f_a + f_b = 0$ and net moment about arbitrary points $\le 10^{-12}$\,N$\cdot$m.
\item Resultant wrench matches sum of individual pair wrenches to $\le 10^{-12}$.
\item Per-pair force traces match \texttt{topology\_side\_wrench} re-evaluated at recorded states to $\le 10^{-9}$\,N across overlap, interlock, and ten-finger styles.
\item Timestep halving ($2 \times 10^{-4}$\,s vs $1 \times 10^{-4}$\,s) changes peak pair normal force by $< 2\%$ ($0.0000\%$ observed across all three styles).
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Limitations.} Hand segments are treated as rigid bodies driven by prescribed kinematics without articulated finger degrees of freedom;
hand--hand pairs are allocated (reporting only, HRG-GS-G3) and do not exert physical forces on the rigid weld kinematics.

\textbf{Reproduction Command.}
\begin{verbatim}
python3 -m pytest tests/unit/grip_contact/test_topology_contact.py \
    tests/unit/grip_contact/test_pinocchio_topology_grip.py
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Batched One-Side Contact Evaluation (HRG-GS-G6 Follow-Up, \#13384)}
\textbf{Purpose.} \texttt{topology\_side\_wrench} sits inside every Radau right-hand-side call of the Pinocchio adapter
(about 3{,}400 calls per $0.04$\,s window), and the per-pair path cost about $1.4$\,ms per side: each pair re-ran the DbC
checks and about 100 small-array numpy operations. The law, its parameters and its tolerances are unchanged; only the
evaluation path changed.

\textbf{Method.} \texttt{topology\_side\_wrench} validates the hand and club \texttt{BushingState} once per side call
(type, finite $3\times3$ rotation with $|R^{\mathsf T}R - I|_{ij} \le 10^{-5}$ and $|\det R - 1| \le 10^{-5}$, finite
$3$-vectors), then evaluates every physical pair of the side in one pass
(\path{src/shared/python/grip_contact/batched_pair_contact.py}, \texttt{side\_rows\_wrench}). The hand-site primitives of a
side and mode are stacked once per \texttt{(side, mode)} and cached on the model (\texttt{TopologyContactModel.side\_batch}).
Per call, two stacked transforms map the hand sites and the grip capsule ends to world axes; each row is then evaluated in
Python floats:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item sphere site $i$ (reference \texttt{sphere\_capsule}): closest grip-axis point $q_i$, normal
  $\hat n_i = (q_i - c_i)/\|q_i - c_i\|$, contact $p_{c,i} = c_i + r_i \hat n_i$; the hand sphere is sampled against the
  plane $(-\hat n_i,\; q_i - r_g \hat n_i)$ with relative velocity $v_h(p_{c,i}) - v_g(p_{c,i})$;
\item capsule site $i$ (reference \texttt{capsule\_capsule}, \#13308): its $N_i$ axial line-contact nodes are stacked as
  sphere rows, and \texttt{combine\_line\_nodes} (shared with the reference) weights each by $1/N_i$ (see Continuous
  Capsule Line-Contact Law); the pair also carries the hand-side moment, since its contact point is a node centroid;
\item \texttt{sphere\_ground\_contact} and \texttt{torsional\_friction\_moment} give the shared law that both rows
  then apply, term for term:
  \begin{gather*}
  f_n = \max\!\big(0,\, k\,\delta\,(1 + d\,\dot\delta)\big), \qquad
  \mu(s) = \mu_d + (\mu_s - \mu_d)e^{-(s/v_{\mathrm{trans}})^2} + \mu_v s, \\
  f_t = -\tfrac{v_t}{s}\,\mu(s)\,f_n \tanh(s/v_{\mathrm{trans}}), \qquad
  M_{\mathrm{spin}} = -\hat u\,\mu_s f_n r_{\mathrm{patch}} \tanh(\omega_n/\omega_0),
  \end{gather*}
  where $s = \|v_t\|$, $\hat u$ is the re-normalised plane normal, and the plane height is taken along the raw normal, as
  \texttt{GroundPlane} does.
\end{itemize}
The side resultant is the left-to-right sum of the pair wrenches on the club, as in the reference loop. Per-pair
\texttt{PairWrench} objects are built only on access (\texttt{\_LazyPairWrenches}). The integrator needs only the resultants
and the force magnitudes.

\textbf{Exactness Rules.} The row kernel reproduces the reference arithmetic operation by operation, so it is a
reformulation and not an approximation:
(i) $R\,x$ uses a stacked \texttt{np.matmul} that issues one BLAS matrix--vector product per row, the same call as the
reference's scalar \texttt{R @ x}. A plain 2-D product (\texttt{P @ R.T}) and a Python-float dot product both differ in the
last bit and were rejected.
(ii) $3$-vector dot products are summed left to right, as numpy's 1-D \texttt{x @ y}, and cross products use the
\texttt{cross3} component expressions.
(iii) Every scalar expression keeps the reference operand order, and the transcendental functions are the same
\texttt{math.exp} and \texttt{math.tanh}.
(iv) The sphere-versus-capsule sign changes are negations ($b - a = -(a - b)$ exactly in IEEE arithmetic).

\textbf{Evidence.} The per-pair path is kept as the reference (\texttt{topology\_side\_wrench\_reference}). Commit
\texttt{8e3b0ff72c} plus this change, on brick (Linux, Python 3.12, numpy 2.5.3, OpenBLAS):
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item Parity test (all three styles, with and without trigger finger, 60 random club poses, hand perturbations of up to
  $0.08$\,rad and $1.5$\,mm, slip-velocity scales $10^{-4}$, $10^{-3}$ and $10^{-1}$\,m/s, plus no-slip and
  Fortran-ordered rotations): every resultant, pair force and moment within $10^{-12}$\,N (N$\cdot$m), and contact geometry
  within $10^{-15}$\,m. Separately, 2{,}400 side evaluations gave a maximum difference of exactly $0$: every resultant and
  every pair wrench was bit-identical.
\item Validation runs once per side call (two \texttt{BushingState} checks), and no per-pair kernel is called (tested).
\item Speed, CPU time (\texttt{time.thread\_time}), best of 9, on a host at load average 12--20 on 4 cores: across the 12
  style/trigger/side cases, the reference took $1437\,\mu$s and the batched path $146\,\mu$s on average, a ratio of means of
  $9.9\times$ (per-case range $8.7$--$13.8\times$). The original PR-head code measured $1337$--$1467\,\mu$s on average, so
  the gain over it is similar. The regression guard asserts $\ge 6\times$.
\item Pinocchio (\path{simulate_grip_contact}, $0.04$\,s window, \path{topology_model}), CPU per case: before
  $10.9$--$13.8$\,s, after $3.8$--$4.8$\,s. Wall time was $5.2$--$11.3$\,s on the loaded host, so
  \path{test_pinocchio_topology_grip.py} stayed in the slow lane at this step (see the next subsection). Its pytest wall times at load average 6--12 are:
  \texttt{\_fast} cases $3.0$--$6.0$\,s, timestep halving $8.6$--$11.0$\,s, $0.2$\,s static hold $12.7$--$14.2$\,s, and
  $1$\,s swing replay $52$--$75$\,s. The non-law right-hand-side costs (prescribed motion, bushing state construction,
  Radau overhead) now dominate.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Failed Experiment.} A row-vectorised numpy pass, with sphere and capsule rows unified by a per-row sign and
selections, matched to $\le 2.3\times10^{-13}$\,N but was only $2.9\times$ faster ($1525 \to 522\,\mu$s). With four to six
rows per side, the roughly 120 numpy calls of $1$--$3\,\mu$s each cost more than the arithmetic, so the Python-float row
kernel replaced it.

\textbf{Limitations.} Bit-identity depends on the BLAS matrix--vector kernel being shared by the stacked and scalar
products; the tested contract is the $10^{-12}$\,N tolerance, not bit-identity. The speedups were measured on a loaded
shared host and vary by about $\pm15\%$ between runs. The G6 parameter line above ($k = 1.6\times10^5$\,N/m, $d = 0.5$\,s/m,
$v_{\mathrm{trans}} = 0.01$\,m/s, $r_{\mathrm{patch}} \approx 4.47$\,mm) does not match the values that
\texttt{build\_pad\_model} returns for the anthropometric driver spec ($k \approx 1.71\times10^5$\,N/m, $d \approx 0.383$\,s/m,
$v_{\mathrm{trans}} = 10^{-3}$\,m/s, $r_{\mathrm{patch}} \approx 2.29$\,mm, $\mu_v = 0$). That discrepancy is recorded for
follow-up and not corrected here.

\textbf{Reproduction Commands.}
\begin{verbatim}
python3 -m pytest -n 0 tests/unit/grip_contact/test_topology_contact_batched.py \
    tests/unit/grip_contact/test_topology_contact.py
python3 -m pytest -n 0 -m slow \
    tests/unit/grip_contact/test_pinocchio_topology_grip.py
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Pinocchio Grip Right-Hand-Side Cost (\#13396)}
\textbf{Purpose.} After the batched law, most of the Pinocchio right-hand side (RHS) was spent outside the contact law.
A profile of the $0.04$\,s overlap swing showed three costs. The weld-model forward kinematics ran about three times
per distinct stage time. \texttt{club\_state} was evaluated twice per RHS. The numpy precondition checks in
\texttt{BushingState} took about 10\% of the RHS. The model, the law, the integrator settings and every tolerance are
unchanged.

\textbf{Method.}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item \textbf{Memoised hand frames.} \texttt{ClubOnBushings.hand\_frames}
  (\path{src/engines/physics_engines/pinocchio/python/grip_bushing.py}) caches the hand bushing frames per
  \texttt{(hand\_source, t)} in a FIFO cache of 8 entries. Radau re-evaluates the RHS at the same stage time across its
  Newton iterations and its 13 finite-difference Jacobian columns, and the hand source (a swing spline or a fixed pose)
  is a pure function of $t$. The cached frames are therefore the frames a fresh evaluation would return. The cache key
  holds the source object, so a recycled \texttt{id} cannot alias an old entry. A shallow copy of the dictionary is
  returned, so callers cannot change the cached entry.
\item \textbf{One club state per RHS.} \texttt{ClubOnBushings.evaluate} returns hands, club state, club frames and the
  law together. \texttt{derivatives} reuses that club state for the joint torque and the translational rate.
  \texttt{ClubInHands} overrides only \texttt{contact\_law}, which dispatches to the pad or topology law.
\item \textbf{Python-float preconditions.} \path{rotation_errors} returns $\max_{ij}|R^{\mathsf T}R - I|_{ij}$ and
  $\det R$ in Python floats (column dot products and cofactor expansion); it is defined in
  \path{src/shared/python/grip_contact/bushing_law.py}. \path{BushingState.__post_init__} keeps its
  $10^{-6}$ orthonormality threshold and its messages. \path{topology_contact._validate_bushing_state} keeps its
  $10^{-5}$ Gram and determinant thresholds and now shares this helper instead of its own copy. Only the precondition
  checks changed; the stored arrays and every computed quantity are untouched.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Evidence.} Brick (Linux, Python 3.12, numpy 2.5.3, OpenBLAS), host at load average 14--28 on 4 cores, CPU time
(\texttt{time.process\_time}):
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item \textbf{Bit-identity.} Six topology runs (overlap, interlock and ten-finger; hold and swing replay; $0.04$\,s) and
  \texttt{simulate\_grip\_bushing} ($0.02$\,s) were compared with a snapshot taken from the code before this change.
  Every output array was bit-identical: the series, pair force traces, hand and club states, and \texttt{nfev}
  (2{,}734, 2{,}631, 2{,}734, 2{,}631, 2{,}437, 2{,}320).
\item \textbf{Weld kinematics.} Calls fell from 3{,}355 to 1{,}039 in the overlap swing case.
\item \textbf{Simulation CPU.} Per $0.04$\,s case it fell from $4.0$--$5.1$\,s to $2.6$--$3.1$\,s, and the six-case total
  from $27.9$\,s to $17.5$\,s ($-37\%$). The DOP853 bushing run gains little ($5.0 \to 4.1$\,s) because an explicit
  integrator does not repeat stage times.
\item \textbf{Pytest CPU.} The six \texttt{\_fast} tests (setup, simulation and the $10^{-9}$\,N trace re-evaluation)
  took $3.2$--$3.7$\,s each. They are below the $5$\,s \texttt{slow} definition, so they now run in the default lane.
  The timestep-halving, $0.2$\,s hold and $1$\,s replay tests stay \texttt{slow}.
\item \textbf{Tests.} \texttt{test\_pinocchio\_hand\_frame\_cache.py} checks that the hand source is called once per
  distinct $t$ over 14 RHS calls per time and that the derivatives equal a fresh, uncached instance bit for bit. It also
  checks that the cache is bounded and isolated. \texttt{test\_bushing\_law.py} checks \texttt{rotation\_errors} against
  numpy on 500 perturbed rotations ($10^{-15}$ Gram, $10^{-14}$ determinant) and pins the $10^{-6}$ threshold on both
  sides.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Limitations.}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item \textbf{Hand-source purity.} The cache is exact only for a hand source that is a pure function of $t$. Both
  adapters pass such sources. A stateful source would need its own cache invalidation.
\item \textbf{Runtime margin.} The default-lane margin is about $1.3$\,s of CPU on this host, so wall time on a loaded
  machine can still exceed $5$\,s. The native Pinocchio lane (\texttt{-m requires\_pinocchio}) runs every case either
  way, and the standard CI unit lane excludes \texttt{requires\_pinocchio}.
\item \textbf{Remaining cost.} The contact law is now about half the RHS. The finite-difference Jacobian is
  \emph{not} a significant cost (see the assessment below).
\item \textbf{Compilation.} The document compiles with pdfTeX 1.40.25 (TeX Live 2023, Debian packages) in two
  \texttt{pdflatex -halt-on-error} passes, with no errors or undefined references (47 pages). Sections up to this
  one set without overfull lines; later grip sections still report overfull lines, which affect layout only.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Reproduction Commands.}
\begin{verbatim}
python3 -m pytest -n 0 tests/unit/grip_contact/test_pinocchio_hand_frame_cache.py \
    tests/unit/grip_contact/test_bushing_law.py \
    tests/unit/grip_contact/test_pinocchio_topology_grip.py
python3 -m pytest -n 0 -m "slow and requires_pinocchio" \
    tests/unit/grip_contact/test_pinocchio_topology_grip.py
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Rejected Follow-Up: Analytic Jacobian (Assessment Only).} Before an analytic $\partial f/\partial y$ was built,
the six $0.04$\,s topology cases were instrumented. SciPy's \texttt{radau.num\_jac} was wrapped for its call count and
CPU time, \texttt{radau.solve\_collocation\_system} for Newton iterations and failures, and \texttt{Radau.\_step\_impl}
for accepted step sizes. Results on brick at load average 8--12:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item \textbf{Jacobian cost.} The finite-difference Jacobian is evaluated 15--54 times per case and costs 5--15\% of CPU.
  Its 13 columns share one stage time, so they reuse the memoised hand frames.
\item \textbf{Newton iterations.} 283--362 steps are accepted per case at 2.10--2.25 simplified-Newton iterations per
  step. Each iteration costs three RHS evaluations, so Newton iterations account for most of the RHS calls. Newton
  failed on 1--17 steps per case.
\item \textbf{Step sizes.} 46--61\% of accepted steps sit at the $2\times10^{-4}$\,s \texttt{max\_step} cap, which is an
  accuracy setting and is not relaxed.
\end{itemize}
An exact Jacobian would remove at most the 5--15\% spent in \texttt{num\_jac}, less the cost of evaluating it. It
cannot reduce the iteration count much below the two iterations Radau needs to confirm convergence, and it could at
best avoid the few Newton-failure step cuts. It would also need derivatives of the regularised friction, the Stribeck
curve and Pinocchio's \texttt{aba}, and it would change the trajectories within the integrator tolerance instead of
reproducing them bit for bit. It was therefore not built. The remaining levers are the per-RHS cost (the contact law
is about half of it) and the number of RHS evaluations, which the step cap and the stiffness fix.

\textbf{Rejected Follow-Up: Inlined Row Kernel (Measured, Reverted).} Unprofiled CPU timing showed where the
simulation time goes: the RHS is about 85\% of simulation CPU, and within it the contact law is about 38\% and
the memoised hand kinematics about 15\%; Radau's own work is about 12\% and setup is negligible. A bit-identical rewrite
of the row kernel was then tried:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item \textbf{Change.} One inlined pair-row function shared by sphere and capsule sites, reading flat per-side
  constants with a single tuple unpack. The validated frame floats were reused instead of being converted twice.
\item \textbf{Bit-identity.} All 3{,}600 side evaluations (every style, trigger, side and mode) matched byte for byte,
  and so did the seven simulation runs.
\item \textbf{Speed.} An isolated contact row fell from $9.1$ to $6.9\,\mu$s. On side calls captured from a simulation
  (every row in contact), timed old against new in one process, the gain was only 2--7\% ($100 \to 95$, $147 \to 137$
  and $145 \to 142\,\mu$s per side), about 2\% of simulation CPU.
\end{itemize}
A side call has no remaining hotspot. Validation, the two stacked transforms, the per-row arithmetic and the output
assembly are each plain CPython bytecode of a few to a few tens of microseconds, so the pure-Python law is at its
floor. The denser kernel was not worth 2--7\% and was reverted. Two levers remain, and both need a decision first:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item \textbf{Compiled row kernel.} Section~F of \texttt{AGENTS.md} admits Rust only for a kernel shared with another
  runtime. Bit-identity would also depend on \texttt{exp} and \texttt{tanh} matching the platform \texttt{libm}.
\item \textbf{Single validation of frame states.} Making the \texttt{BushingState} arrays read-only would let the
  topology law trust the construction-time checks, worth about 10--15\% of the law. It changes the behaviour of a type
  shared across engines.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Continuous Capsule Line-Contact Law (HRG-GS-G10, \#13308)}
\label{sec:hrg-gs-line-contact}
\textbf{Problem.} The capsule pair law (\texttt{capsule\_capsule}) used the closest points of the two core segments
(\texttt{closest\_points\_segments}). Their minimum distance is continuous, but the closest points are not: for nearly
parallel segments the routine returned the overlap centre while $\sin^2\theta = a e - b^2 < 10^{-10}\,a e$ and an endpoint
just past it. For the \texttt{ten\_finger} trail little-finger capsule ($r = 7.5$\,mm, $L = 16$\,mm, preload
$\delta_0 = 0.25$\,mm) at a pitch of $0.99 \times 10^{-5}$ versus $1.01 \times 10^{-5}$\,rad, the contact point moved from
$x = -48.0$ to $-56.0$\,mm and the club moment changed from $2.053$ to $2.396$\,N$\cdot$m. Physically, a
penetrating capsule nearly parallel to a cylinder makes a line contact whose resultant lies near the overlap centre and
moves continuously as the capsule tilts.

\textbf{Law.} Capsule A's core segment $[p_0, p_1]$, of length $L$ and radius $r_A$, is discretised into
$N = \max(2, \mathrm{round}(L / (r_A/2)) + 1)$ equally spaced nodes (\texttt{line\_contact\_node\_count},
\texttt{capsule\_line\_nodes}):
\begin{equation}
c_k = p_0 + \frac{k}{N-1}(p_1 - p_0), \qquad k = 0, \dots, N-1.
\end{equation}
Each node is a sphere $(c_k, r_A)$ evaluated against capsule B by the shared \texttt{sphere\_capsule} law (Hunt--Crossley
normal, regularised Coulomb/Stribeck friction and torsional spin friction), giving the full-stiffness node wrench
$(\mathbf{F}_k, \mathbf{M}_k)$ with depth $\delta_k = r_A + r_B - d(c_k, [q_0, q_1])$. The pair wrench is
(\texttt{combine\_line\_nodes})
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{F} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_k \mathbf{F}_k, \qquad \mathbf{M} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_k \mathbf{M}_k,
\end{equation}
which is the line contact $\mathbf{F} = \int_0^1 \mathbf{f}(s)\,ds$ of per-length stiffness $k/L$, evaluated by an
$N$-point rule. The law is linear in $k$ (the normal force $\max(0, k\delta(1 + d\dot\delta))$, the friction and the spin
moment all scale with it), so weighting the full-stiffness node wrenches by $1/N$ equals running every node at $k/N$.
Reported quantities: $f_n = \tfrac1N\sum_k f_{n,k}$ and $f_t = \tfrac1N\sum_k f_{t,k}$ (line loads, as MuJoCo and
OpenSim sum their per-contact magnitudes), $\delta = \max_k \delta_k$, contact point
$\mathbf{p} = \sum_k f_{n,k}\mathbf{p}_k / \sum_k f_{n,k}$ (the normal-force-weighted node centroid), and normal
$\hat{\mathbf{n}} \propto \sum_k f_{n,k}\hat{\mathbf{n}}_k$. With zero total normal force, $\mathbf{p}$ and
$\hat{\mathbf{n}}$ fall back to the unweighted node means.

\textbf{Properties.}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item \emph{Continuity.} Every node wrench is continuous in the poses, and no closest point or branch is selected, so
  $\mathbf{F}$, $\mathbf{M}$, $f_n$ and $\mathbf{p}$ (where $f_n > 0$) are continuous. A node whose end lifts off loses
  its load smoothly.
\item \emph{Parallel equivalence.} With a uniform depth $\delta_0$ along an exactly parallel capsule, all nodes carry
  $k\delta_0$, so $f_n = k\delta_0$ at the overlap centre: the closest-point law's result at $\theta = 0$. Preload
  calibrations of every engine are therefore unchanged.
\item \emph{Linear depth.} For a small pitch in the plane of the two axes with both ends in contact, $\delta(s)$ is linear and symmetric nodes give
  $\tfrac1N\sum_k\delta_k = \delta(\tfrac12)$ exactly, so the total load is unchanged by the tilt while the centroid moves
  toward the deeper end.
\item \emph{Action and reaction} hold node by node, so they hold for the sum; each node is unilateral and inside its
  friction cone at its own slip speed.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Engine Mirrors (Single Source).} Pinocchio, Drake and the OpenSim MSK adapter call
\texttt{topology\_side\_wrench}, whose batched kernel (\texttt{side\_rows\_wrench}) stacks the capsule nodes as sphere rows
and combines them with the same \texttt{combine\_line\_nodes}. OpenSim's topology grip already modelled each capsule as
this sphere chain with stiffness $k/N$; \texttt{capsule\_sphere\_count} is now the shared \texttt{line\_contact\_node\_count}.
MuJoCo emits one sphere geom per node, each paired with the grip cylinder under the site's calibrated \texttt{solref}.
\texttt{ClubInHands.calibrate} sums the node contacts of a site, so the per-site calibration is unchanged. MuJoCo's
analytic sphere--cylinder collider replaces the capsule--cylinder \texttt{mjc\_Convex} path, and
\texttt{\_sync\_parallel\_capsules} is deleted.
MuJoCo's regularised friction gain belongs to each contact row and does not scale with \texttt{solref}, so $N$ node rows
at the single-contact \texttt{solreffriction} time constant $T_f$ resisted slip about $N$ times as hard as the shared law:
$37.94$\,N versus $19.49$\,N of tangential force on the trail little finger at $t = 10$\,ms of the \texttt{ten\_finger}
hold (\texttt{pair\_tangential\_force} $75.5\% / 81.6\%$ peak/RMS error at $0.04$\,s). Each node row therefore uses
$N T_f$ (a sphere site keeps $T_f$): $22.65$ versus $21.44$\,N at $10$\,ms and $8.38$ versus $9.64$\,N at $30$\,ms, and the
pair's tangential error falls to $3.65\% / 3.07\%$.

\textbf{Evidence.}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item \emph{Continuity} (\path{tests/unit/grip_contact/test_capsule_line_contact.py}): the contact point moves less than
  $10^{-7}$\,m between $\theta = 0.99 \times 10^{-5}$ and $1.01 \times 10^{-5}$\,rad (formerly $8.0$\,mm); over 801
  tilts in $[-10^{-3}, 10^{-3}]$\,rad no step exceeds $2.5\,\mu$m; the normal force over 401 tilts in $[0, 0.2]$\,rad
  never steps by more than $5\%$ of its preload value; at $\theta = 0$ the force is $k\delta_0$ at the overlap centre
  to $10^{-12}$ relative.
\item \emph{Mirrors}: the batched kernel equals the reference \texttt{capsule\_capsule} to $10^{-12}$\,N
  (\texttt{test\_topology\_contact\_batched.py}); MuJoCo's native node forces are uniform and linear in depth
  ($34{,}220$\,N/m per node at preload) and their total matches the shared law to $0.004\%$ at $10$\,ms of the hold;
  the load-weighted node centroid stays within $0.5$\,mm of the shared contact point before and after the old
  threshold crossing (\texttt{test\_capsule\_line\_contact\_continuous\_and\_matches\_shared\_law\_ten\_finger}); and
  node friction tracks the law to $10\%$ (\texttt{test\_capsule\_node\_friction\_matches\_shared\_line\_law\_ten\_finger}).
\item \emph{Cross-engine parity} (\texttt{grip\_style\_parity\_report.py}; tolerances unchanged at $5\%$ peak and $2\%$
  RMS). \texttt{overlap} and \texttt{interlock} contain no hand--grip capsule and are unchanged. Drake (shared kernel)
  passes every case: \texttt{ten\_finger} \texttt{net\_force} $0.218\% / 0.187\%$ hold and $0.015\% / 1.185\%$ swing at
  $0.04$\,s. MuJoCo passes \texttt{ten\_finger} swing at $0.04$\,s ($2.244\% / 1.832\%$) and both motions at $0.10$\,s
  ($1.873\%$ and $1.292\%$ RMS), but fails the $0.04$\,s hold: \texttt{net\_force} $3.136\% / 2.866\%$ (strict
  \texttt{xfail}; previously $1.902\% / 1.971\%$). The per-row friction gain schedule of \#13306 (Section~\ref{sec:hrg-gs-mujoco-topology}) then closed
  this gap ($0.309\% / 0.413\%$) and the strict \texttt{xfail} was removed. OpenSim's $0.04$\,s \texttt{ten\_finger} cases stay strict
  \texttt{xfail}s. With the G7b mesh changes of \#13458 they read $4.639\% / 4.577\%$ hold and $3.682\% / 2.374\%$ swing
  (\#13458 measured $2.395\% / 2.588\%$ and $2.499\% / 2.605\%$ against the closest-point reference). Over $0.10$\,s the
  hold fails at $4.639\% / 2.970\%$ (it passed at $1.716\%$ RMS against the closest-point reference) and the swing passes
  at $3.682\% / 1.911\%$.
\item \emph{Diagnosis of the MuJoCo and OpenSim hold regressions.} The new reference is physically different: the
  former endpoint snap at $1.18$\,ms dropped $13.5\%$ of the little-finger pitch couple; without it the club's
  pitch transient is about $15\%$ larger (Pinocchio $|F_{\mathrm{net}}|$ peak $3.571$ versus $3.489$\,N; little-finger
  $f_n$ now decays from $42.77$ to $40.98$\,N, as OpenSim's sphere chain already did, instead of staying at
  $42.66$\,N). Every MuJoCo pair force is closer to the reference than before (RMS difference $0.025$--$0.124$\,N
  versus $0.038$--$0.143$\,N for the nine hand--grip pads), yet the club \texttt{net\_force}, the $\approx 3.5$\,N
  residual of $\approx 1$\,kN of opposing grip forces, carries the same phase-shifted oscillation error as before at
  $1.6\times$ the amplitude ($0.107$ versus $0.069$\,N RMS). This is the hyperstatic cone-QP split of \#13306, not a
  mismatch of the capsule law.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Limitations.}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item A capsule crossing the partner at a large angle touches only the nodes near the crossing, so its load is about
  $k\delta \cdot w/L$ for a contact width $w$, smaller than the former point law's $k\delta$. Every capsule pair in the
  current topologies is axial, so no current result changes for this reason; a crossing site would need its own stiffness.
\item The $N$-point rule is exact for a linear depth profile. After one end lifts off, it approximates
  $\int\delta^+\,ds$ with an error below one node spacing ($\approx r/2$) of the lift-off point.
\item Nodes sit on capsule A's axis only, so the law is not symmetric in A and B.
\item \texttt{capsule\_capsule\_distance} and \texttt{closest\_points\_segments} keep the closest-point geometry for gaps
  and distances. \texttt{hand\_hand\_allocation} still takes its allocation patch from that geometry; the hand-hand
  relative pose is constant to $10^{-10}$\,mm in the captured swing, so it does not jump there.
\item MuJoCo's $0.04$\,s \texttt{ten\_finger} hold and OpenSim's $0.10$\,s \texttt{ten\_finger} hold fail \texttt{net\_force}
  RMS against the continuous reference (above). The tolerances were not changed; closing them needs the \#13306
  hyperstatic friction work, not a capsule-law change.
\item This document was not compiled locally (no \texttt{pdflatex} on the host).
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Reproduction Commands.}
\begin{verbatim}
python3 -m pytest -n 0 tests/unit/grip_contact/test_capsule_line_contact.py \
    tests/unit/grip_contact/test_capsule_contact_law.py \
    tests/unit/grip_contact/test_topology_contact_batched.py
python3 -m pytest -n 0 tests/unit/grip_contact/test_mujoco_topology_grip.py
PYTHONPATH=. python3 scripts/grip/grip_style_parity_report.py --duration-s 0.04 --out out/g10_004
PYTHONPATH=. python3 scripts/grip/grip_style_parity_report.py --duration-s 0.10 --out out/g10_010
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{MuJoCo Topology Contact Reference (HRG-GS-G8, \#13306)}
\label{sec:hrg-gs-mujoco-topology}
\textbf{Topology Geometry and Pair Generation.} The MuJoCo grip-contact adapter (\path{src/engines/physics_engines/mujoco/python/grip_contact_sim.py})
accepts an optional \texttt{topology\_model: TopologyContactModel} in \texttt{contact\_mjcf}, \texttt{ClubInHands}, \texttt{build\_run}, \texttt{hold\_run}, and \texttt{simulate\_grip\_contact}.
When \texttt{topology\_model} is supplied, the legacy symmetric 6+6 sphere \texttt{PadLayout} is replaced by one MuJoCo collision primitive per physical hand--grip pair in \texttt{topology\_model.physical\_grip\_pairs(side)}:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item Spherical pad sites (\texttt{primitive="sphere"}) emit \texttt{<geom name="\{site\}" type="sphere" size="\{r\}" pos="\{p\}" contype="0" conaffinity="0" mass="0"/>}.
\item Cylindrical phalanx sites (\texttt{primitive="capsule"}) emit, since \#13308, one sphere geom \texttt{\{site\}\_node\{j\}} of radius $r$ per shared axial line-contact node (\texttt{capsule\_line\_nodes}), each with its own \texttt{<pair>} under the site's calibrated \texttt{solref}; the original single \texttt{type="capsule"} geom is described below as a superseded root cause.
\end{itemize}
Each site primitive is placed on \texttt{hand\_L} (\texttt{lead}) or \texttt{hand\_R} (\texttt{trail}) by reusing \texttt{map\_site\_to\_hand\_frame(topology, pair.site\_a, side)} without introducing new coordinate frame transformations.
All contact geoms carry \texttt{contype="0" conaffinity="0"}, so broadphase parent--child and hand--hand collisions are disabled; in addition, \texttt{<exclude body1="hand\_L" body2="hand\_R"/>} is emitted in \texttt{<contact>} and one explicit \texttt{<pair geom1="\{site\}" geom2="grip" condim="6" .../>} is generated per physical hand--grip site.
Hand--hand pairs (\texttt{hand\_hand\_pairs()}) are never emitted as physical MuJoCo contact pairs; instead, \texttt{PairTraceRecorder} records their normal and tangential force channels as \texttt{NaN} with explanatory reasons in \texttt{ContactRun.channel\_reasons}.

\textbf{Adapter Diagnosis and Root-Cause Fixes.} Law-level and transient-settling diagnostics identified six physical/geometric root causes:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item \textit{Uncompensated hand gravity and missing prescribed hand acceleration in \texttt{place\_hands}}: Because \texttt{hand\_L} and \texttt{hand\_R} are \texttt{<freejoint/>} bodies ($m_{\mathrm{hand}} = 100$\,kg, $I_{\mathrm{hand}} = 10\,\mathrm{kg\cdot m^2} I_3$) with prescribed state, leaving \texttt{qfrc\_applied} $= 0$ caused MuJoCo's Delassus solver to use unconstrained hand acceleration $\ddot{q}_{\mathrm{hand},0} = [0, 0, -g, 0, 0, 0]$ inside \texttt{mj\_forward} and \texttt{mj\_step}. Setting \texttt{qfrc\_applied} on \texttt{hand\_L} and \texttt{hand\_R} to $m_{\mathrm{hand}}(a_{\mathrm{lin}} - g)$ and $I_{\mathrm{hand}} a_{\mathrm{ang}}$ (with velocity evaluated at time $t$ consistent with \texttt{mj\_Euler}) eliminates uncompensated hand-gravity drift and supplies the prescribed kinematic hand acceleration to the Delassus right-hand side.
\item \textit{Parasitic normal constraint mass and acceleration leak $\epsilon_n = d_0 / (1 - d_0)$}: In MuJoCo's Delassus equation $(A + R) f = -J \ddot{q}_0 - a_{\mathrm{ref}}$ with direct $\mathrm{solref} = (-k_s, -b_s)$ and $\mathrm{solimp}$ impedance $d_0$, multiplying the normal rows by $d_0 / (1 - d_0)$ yields
\begin{equation}
\left(\hat{A}_n + \frac{d_0}{1 - d_0} A_{nn}\right) f_n + \frac{d_0}{1 - d_0} A_{nt} f_t = \frac{k_s \delta + b_s \dot{\delta}}{1 - d_0} - \frac{d_0}{1 - d_0} J_n \ddot{q}_0.
\end{equation}
At $d_0 = 0.95$, the regularizer added $53.4$\,kg of artificial normal constraint mass; at $d_0 = 0.01$, $\epsilon_n = d_0/(1-d_0) = 1.01\%$ still leaked $1\%$ of unconstrained relative acceleration $J_n \ddot{q}_0$ (gravity plus prescribed swing hand acceleration $a_{\mathrm{hand}}(0)$) into $f_n$ at $t = 0$, producing a $4.294\%$ $t = 0$ \texttt{net\_moment\_com} error in \texttt{ten\_finger} swing. Setting $d_0 = 0.001$ (\texttt{TOPOLOGY\_IMPEDANCE}) reduces the acceleration leak by $10\times$ to $\epsilon_n = 0.100\%$, dropping the $t=0$ \texttt{net\_moment\_com} error in \texttt{ten\_finger} swing from $4.294\%$ to $0.265\%$ and in \texttt{ten\_finger} hold from $2.398\%$ to $0.012\%$.
\item \textit{Missing analytical \texttt{mjc\_CapsuleCylinder} collider (\texttt{mjc\_Convex} \texttt{multiccd} contact jumping)}: MuJoCo dispatches \texttt{capsule}--\texttt{cylinder} pairs to \texttt{mjc\_Convex}, which with \texttt{multiccd} enabled emitted 3 contacts at $t=0$, dropped to 2 at $\theta \ge 5.6$\,nrad, and 1 at $\theta \ge 1.71$\,mrad on \texttt{ten\_finger\_little\_finger\_grip}. Emitting \texttt{<flag multiccd="disable" refsafe="disable"/>} on the topology path enforces a single contact across all orientations.
\item \textit{Collinear capsule--cylinder contact jump (superseded by \#13308)}: In \texttt{ten\_finger}, \texttt{trail\_little\_finger} is a $16.0$\,mm capsule ($r = 7.5$\,mm, half-length $8.0$\,mm) whose axis is parallel to the grip cylinder axis at $t = 0$ and opposes \texttt{lead\_heel\_pad\_butt} across a $62.0$\,mm axial lever arm (a pure $2.5340$\,N$\cdot$m pitch couple while radial forces cancel). The former closest-point law (\texttt{closest\_points\_segments}) placed the contact at the overlap centre while $\sin^2\theta < 10^{-10}$ ($|\theta| < 10^{-5}$\,rad, the first $118$ steps, $t \le 1.18$\,ms) and at the proximal endpoint just past it, an $8.0$\,mm jump; \texttt{mjc\_Convex} snapped by $8.000$\,mm already at step $1$ ($|\theta| \sim 10^{-8}$\,rad), cutting the pitch couple by $42.7738\,\mathrm{N} \times 0.008\,\mathrm{m} = 0.3422$\,N$\cdot$m ($13.5\%$). \emph{Failed experiment:} \texttt{ClubInHands.\_sync\_parallel\_capsules} shrank the capsule geom to a $10^{-9}$\,m half-length while $\sin^2\theta < 10^{-10}$, so MuJoCo copied the shared law's discontinuity instead of removing it. Both were replaced by the continuous line-contact law and node spheres (\#13308); the workaround is deleted.
\item \textit{Tangential viscous friction scaling (\texttt{impratio} / \texttt{solreffriction})}: Tangential viscous damping $c_{\mathrm{fric}} = B_f/R_f = \frac{2\,\mathrm{impratio}}{(1-d_0)t_f \hat{A}_0}$ depends only on $\mathrm{impratio}/t_f$, whereas parasitic tangential mass $1/R_f \propto d_0 \cdot \mathrm{impratio}$. With $d_0 = 0.001$, using $\mathrm{impratio} = 0.95$ (\texttt{TOPOLOGY\_IMPRATIO\_STATIC}) and $t_f = 19\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ (\texttt{TOPOLOGY\_FRICTION\_TIME\_STATIC\_S}) for un-stepped static \texttt{mj\_forward} ($1/R_f = 0.27$\,g) and scaling both by $1000\times$ ($\mathrm{impratio} = 950$, $t_f = 19$\,ms) during \texttt{advance()} preserves $\mathrm{impratio}/t_f = 5.0 \times 10^4\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$ and $d_0 t_f = 1.9\Delta t > \Delta t$.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Per-Row Friction Gain Schedule (\#13306).} A fixed \texttt{solreffriction} time constant $t_f$ gives every
friction row one viscous gain $c = B_f/R_f$ with $B_f = 2/(d_{\max} t_f)$, whereas the shared regularised law gives
$|\mathbf{f}_t| = g(v)\,f_n\,v$ with the secant slip gain
\begin{equation}
g(v) = \frac{\mu(v)\tanh(v/v_{\mathrm{tr}})}{v}, \qquad \lim_{v \to 0} g(v) = \frac{\mu_s}{v_{\mathrm{tr}}}
\end{equation}
(\texttt{friction\_secant\_gain} in \path{src/shared/python/motion_matching/contact_kernel.py}), which depends on each
row's normal force and slip speed. After $10$\,ms of the \texttt{ten\_finger} hold, the fixed $t_f$ left the native friction
of loaded node rows $0.957$--$1.166\times$ the law at their own $(f_n, v)$, and no global constant (the sweeps of $t_f$,
\texttt{impratio}, the step scale, \texttt{condim}, $d_0$ and $\Delta t$ in the \#13306 investigation) brought the $0.04$\,s
hold \texttt{net\_force} below $2.5\%$ RMS. \texttt{ClubInHands.advance} therefore sets, before every step, each loaded node
pair's time constant from the previous step's row state:
\begin{equation}
t_{f,i} = \frac{2}{d_{\max,i}\, g(v_i)\, f_{n,i}\, R_{f,i}},
\end{equation}
with $f_{n,i}$, $v_i = \|(\dot{e}_{i,1}, \dot{e}_{i,2})\|$ and $R_{f,i}$ read from \texttt{efc\_force}, \texttt{efc\_vel} and
\texttt{efc\_R} (a one-step lag at $\Delta t = 10\,\mu$s); unloaded rows keep their last value. Leaving the stepped regime
(\texttt{\_set\_step\_scaling(False)}, before calibration and static evaluation) restores the static time constants exactly.
The loaded rows then carry $0.989$--$1.002\times$ the law's friction, and the determinate \texttt{net\_force} error falls as
follows (peak / RMS, scheduled run against the Pinocchio reference):
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item \texttt{ten\_finger} hold, $0.04$\,s: $3.136\% / 2.866\% \to 0.309\% / 0.413\%$; $0.10$\,s: $3.136\% / 1.873\% \to 0.309\% / 0.267\%$.
\item \texttt{ten\_finger} swing, $0.04$\,s: $2.244\% / 1.832\% \to 0.248\% / 0.270\%$.
\item \texttt{overlap} and \texttt{interlock}, $0.04$\,s: hold $0.009\% / 0.137\%$, swing $0.027\% / 0.137\%$.
\end{itemize}
The schedule reproduces the law's force at the row's slip; it does not remove the Delassus coupling or the elliptic-cone
normal-force inflation listed below, which act through the slip each row develops.

\textbf{Per-Site \texttt{solref} Calibration.} MuJoCo's negative-\texttt{solref} direct stiffness/damping parameterization is initialized per physical site $i$ on the topology path as
\begin{equation}
k_{0} = \frac{4 k}{d_0^2}, \quad b_i = k_{\mathrm{solref},i} \, \delta_0 \, d, \quad \mathrm{solref}_i = (-k_{\mathrm{solref},i}, -b_i),
\end{equation}
where $k = 1.711 \times 10^5$\,N/m is the shared penalty stiffness, $d = 0.3827$\,s/m is the dissipation constant, $d_0 = 0.001$ is the topology \texttt{solimp} impedance at preload $\delta_0 = 0.25$\,mm, and $\mathrm{solimp} = (0.001, 0.001, 0.001, 0.5, 2)$.
\texttt{ClubInHands.calibrate(q, iterations=8, tol=1e-3, target\_force=...)} scales each site's stiffness against native \texttt{mj\_contactForce} after plain \texttt{mj\_forward}.

\textbf{Measured Static and Dynamic Parity ($0.10$\,s Window).} Table~\ref{tab:mujoco-topology-parity} summarizes the measured native static force accuracy (at $1\times$ preload $\delta = 0.25$\,mm and $2\times$ preload $\delta = 0.50$\,mm using the $1\times$-calibrated \texttt{solref}), the determinate net club wrench parity ($F_{\mathrm{net}}$ and $M_{\mathrm{com}}$ about the club CoM over $0.10$\,s, all passing $\le 5\%$ peak and $\le 2\%$ RMS), and the indeterminate per-hand force split (\texttt{force\_L}, \texttt{force\_R}) against the Pinocchio G6 topology reference across all three grip styles ($1100$\,N squeeze per hand).

Across all 10 \texttt{PARITY\_QUANTITIES} on the $0.10$\,s window, the overall maximum $(\text{peak\_error}, \text{rms\_error})$ is $(35.3\%, 53.1\%)$ for \texttt{overlap}/\texttt{interlock} hold (previously $(35.8\%, 53.1\%)$), $(39.5\%, 35.8\%)$ for \texttt{ten\_finger} hold (previously $(33.6\%, 29.9\%)$ under the closest-point capsule law), $(38.2\%, 68.2\%)$ for \texttt{overlap}/\texttt{interlock} swing (previously $(38.4\%, 68.3\%)$), and $(82.7\%, 72.4\%)$ for \texttt{ten\_finger} swing (previously $(94.7\%, 67.2\%)$). The \texttt{ten\_finger} row is measured with the continuous line-contact law and per-node friction scaling of \#13308 (Section~\ref{sec:hrg-gs-line-contact}).
These maxima predate the per-row friction gain schedule. With the schedule the $0.10$\,s maxima are $(3.89\%, 4.60\%)$ for
\texttt{overlap}/\texttt{interlock} hold and $(2.99\%, 3.39\%)$ for swing, both from \texttt{deflection\_R} (per-hand forces
$\le 0.39\%$ peak and $0.78\%$ RMS), and $(2.01\%, 0.71\%)$ for \texttt{ten\_finger} hold and $(1.86\%, 1.05\%)$ for swing, both
from \texttt{squeeze}: all ten quantities of \texttt{ten\_finger} hold and swing pass.

\textbf{Hyperstatic Per-Hand Split Analysis and Minimal Two-Pad Demonstration (\#13306).} Per \path{docs/development/full_body_models/GRIP_PARITY_DECISIONS.md} Section~19, the per-hand force split of a hyperstatic two-hand grip is governed by the internal compliance nullspace and agrees across engines only if both apply the identical compliant force law at every pad. Mathematical analysis and a minimal two-pad experiment (\texttt{test\_two\_pad\_hyperstatic\_penalty\_vs\_mujoco\_cone\_divergence} in \path{tests/unit/grip_contact/test_mujoco_topology_grip.py}: lead sphere $\mathrm{Pad}_L$ at $(-x_{\mathrm{pad}}, 0, +0.03)$\,m and trail sphere $\mathrm{Pad}_R$ at $(+x_{\mathrm{pad}}, 0, -0.03)$\,m on a $0.313$\,kg club cylinder of radius $14$\,mm at preload $\delta_0 = 0.25$\,mm, $f_{n,0} = 42.774$\,N) prove that MuJoCo's constraint solver cannot represent the shared penalty law per pad under any fixed configuration (\texttt{condim=3} or \texttt{6}, \texttt{elliptic} or \texttt{pyramidal} cone, with or without spin/rolling friction) for four structural reasons (the per-row friction gain schedule above changes \texttt{solreffriction} every step, so it lies outside this set and removes the fourth):
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,noitemsep]
\item \textit{Delassus Inertia Coupling vs.\ Local Penalty Independence}: In the shared penalty law, each pad's wrench is a purely local function of $(\delta_i, \dot{\delta}_i, \mathbf{v}_{t,i})$. In MuJoCo, all contacts on the club are solved simultaneously via $(J M^{-1} J^T + R) f = -J \ddot{q}_0 - a_{\mathrm{ref}}$. During time-stepping ($\mathrm{impratio} = 950$, $R_t = 3.34\,\mathrm{kg}^{-1}$ vs.\ collective tangential Delassus eigenvalue $\lambda_A \approx 32\text{--}184\,\mathrm{kg}^{-1}$), off-diagonal blocks $A_{ij} = J_i M_{\mathrm{club}}^{-1} J_j^T$ couple all pads and suppress per-pad tangential force at State~1 ($v_x = +20$\,mm/s, $v_z = -0.5$\,mm/s) from $16.786$\,N / $17.045$\,N (shared law) to $5.176$\,N / $5.598$\,N in MuJoCo.
\item \textit{Elliptic Friction-Cone QCQP Normal-Force Inflation}: In the shared penalty law, normal force $f_n = k \delta \operatorname{clamp}(1 + c\dot{\delta}, 0, 3)$ is strictly independent of tangential slip $\mathbf{v}_t$. In MuJoCo's elliptic cone solver, when unconstrained tangential damping $f_{t,\mathrm{unc}} = (B_f / R_t)\|\mathbf{v}_t\|$ exceeds $\mu f_{n,0}$, the QCQP projects $(f_{n,0}, f_{t,\mathrm{unc}})$ onto $\|f_t\| \le \mu f_n$ in the metric $\operatorname{diag}(R_n, R_t)$ with $R_n / R_t = \mathrm{impratio}$, inflating the normal force to
\begin{equation}
f_n = \frac{\mathrm{impratio}\cdot f_{n,0} + \mu\, f_{t,\mathrm{unc}}}{\mathrm{impratio} + \mu^2}, \qquad f_t = \mu f_n.
\end{equation}
At State~2 ($v_z = -3.0\,\mathrm{mm/s} = 3 v_{\mathrm{tr}}$), the shared law keeps $f_n = 42.774$\,N and saturates $f_t = 29.795$\,N ($\|\mathbf{f}_L\| = 52.128$\,N), whereas MuJoCo (\texttt{condim=3} or \texttt{6}, \texttt{cone="elliptic"}, $\mathrm{impratio} = 0.95$) inflates $f_n$ to $70.941$\,N ($+65.9\%$ squeeze error) and $f_t$ to $63.846$\,N ($+83.1\%$ $\|\mathbf{f}_L\|$ error); at $\mathrm{impratio} = 1.0$, $f_n = 70.160$\,N ($+64.0\%$).
\item \textit{Pyramidal Cone Normal-Force Bias}: Switching to \texttt{cone="pyramidal"} decomposes contact into facet rows $f_n \pm \mu f_{t,k} \ge 0$ whose combined normal compliance shifts the static normal force at State~0 ($\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{0}$) from $42.774$\,N to $55.432$\,N ($+29.6\%$ error) and collapses tangential force at State~1 to $0.004$\,N.
\item \textit{Linear Constraint Friction vs.\ Nonlinear Stribeck Curve and Bilinear Normal Damping}: MuJoCo's \texttt{solreffriction} provides a constant linear tangential rate $B_f \mathbf{v}_t$ clipped at a single friction coefficient $\mu_s = 0.9$, whereas the shared law evaluates $\mu(\|\mathbf{v}_t\|) = [\mu_d + (\mu_s - \mu_d)e^{-(\|\mathbf{v}_t\|/v_{\mathrm{tr}})^2}]\tanh(\|\mathbf{v}_t\|/v_{\mathrm{tr}})$ with $\mu_d = 0.7 \ne \mu_s = 0.9$, and scales normal damping bilinearly with instantaneous $\delta(t)$ rather than fixed $\delta_0$. The per-row friction gain schedule (\#13306) sets $B_f$ from $g(v)$ every step and so removes the friction half of this item; the normal-damping half remains.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{Minimal Two-Pad Hyperstatic Comparison: Shared Penalty Law vs.\ MuJoCo Configurations (\#13306)}
\label{tab:mujoco-two-pad-hyperstatic}

\midrule
